by repeatedly using the definition of the curvature tensor and the fact that since
[J_{2},\nabla r]|_{\gamma}=0
, we have that
\nabla_{J_{2}}\nabla r=\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}J_{2}=\dot{J}_{2}

\displaystyle\nabla_{J_{2}}\nabla_{\nabla r}\nabla_{\nabla r}\tilde{J}_{1}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\nabla_{J_{2}}\nabla_{\nabla r}\tilde{J}_{1}+R(J_{2},\dot{\gamma})\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}J_{1}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}K+\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\lparen R\lparen J_{2},\dot{\gamma}\rparen J_{1}\rparen+R(J_{2},\dot{\gamma})\dot{J}_{1}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}K+\lparen\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}R\rparen\lparen J_{2},\dot{\gamma}\rparen J_{1}+R\lparen\dot{J}_{2},\dot{\gamma}\rparen J_{1}+2R\lparen J_{2},\dot{\gamma}\rparen\dot{J}_{1}.

We can expand the second term as

\nabla_{J_{2}}\lparen R(\tilde{J}_{1},\nabla r)\nabla r\rparen=\lparen\nabla_{J_{2}}R\rparen\lparen J_{1},\dot{\gamma}\rparen\dot{\gamma}+R\lparen K,\dot{\gamma}\rparen\dot{\gamma}+R\lparen J_{1},\dot{J}_{2}\rparen\dot{\gamma}+R\lparen J_{1},\dot{\gamma}\rparen\dot{J}_{2}.

Putting everything together and using the symmetries of the curvature tensor and the first Bianchi identity we get the differential equation.

For the initial conditions, we have that

K(0)=\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{0}(0,0)=0.

Differentiating the Hessian in the direction of
\dot{\gamma}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
we have that

\dot{K}(0)=\lparen\nabla\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{0}(v,0,0)+\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{0}(w_{1},0)+\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{0}(0,w_{2})=0.\qed

5.4.2

Manifolds of bounded geometry

By
Proposition

5.4.1
, we have that the Hessian of the exponential map solves a Jacobi-like differential equation with two main differences: It is inhomogeneous and depends on the covariant derivative of the curvature tensor.

The inhomogeneity of the differential equation will stop us from simplifying the differential equation into two first order differential equations, as we did for the Jacobi equation. In contrast, this time we will have to deal directly with the second order equation. Luckily, we have already developed most of the tools to do so.

The fact that involves the covariant derivative of the curvature tensor is a more intrinsic difference. Since the sectional curvature completely defines the curvature tensor, bounds on the sectional curvature can be translated into bounds on the norm of the curvature tensor. One question that naturally arises is whether these
0
-th order bounds also give first order bounds. As one may expect, this is not the case.

Example 5.4.2
(Manifold of bounded
0
-th order and unbounded
1
-st order).

Consider a rotationally symmetric surface of the form
\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\rho(r)^{2}\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}
on the cylinder
(0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
for a function
\rho>0
. These metrics have a particularly simple formula for the sectional curvature

\sec(\partial_{r},\partial_{\theta})=\frac{R(\partial_{r},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{r})}{\rho^{2}}=-\frac{\ddot{\rho}}{\rho}.

All we have to do now is to choose a positive function
\rho
with bounded second derivative and arbitrarily large third derivative, for example
\rho(r)=2+r^{5}\sin(1/r)
. This metric has
\lvert\sec\rvert<12
, but

\lparen\nabla_{\partial_{r}}R\rparen\lparen\partial_{r},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{r}\rparen=D_{\partial_{r}}\lparen R\lparen\partial_{r},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{r}\rparen\rparen-2R\lparen\partial_{r},\nabla_{\partial_{r}}\partial_{\theta},\partial_{\theta},\partial_{r}\rparen=\dot{\rho}\ddot{\rho}-\rho{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rho}\limits^{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\vbox to-2.2pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.89}{.}\kern-0.5pt\scalebox{.89}{.}\kern-0.5pt\scalebox{.89}{.}}\vss}}}}

which is unbounded as
r
goes down to
0
.

This example motivates the following definition.

Definition 5.4.3
(Manifold of bounded geometry).

Let
(M,\textsl{g})
be a Riemannian manifold. We say that a manifold has
k
-bounded geometry
if for every
i=0,\dots,k
there exist constants
C_{i}\geq 0
such that

\lVert\nabla^{i}R\rVert\leq C_{i}

and the injectivity radius is uniformly bounded below by a positive constant

\inj\coloneqq\inf_{p\in M}\inj(p)>0.

Remark 5.4.4
.

A manifold has
0
-bounded geometry if it has bounded sectional curvature above and below and positive injectivity radius. As one may expect, given that the
1
-bounded geometry is given by the Christoffel symbols, which are given by directional derivatives of the metric
g
in normal coordinates, the condition on the boundedness of the derivatives of the curvature tensor is equivalent to asking for the entries of the metric tensor to be bounded in normal coordinates. This was first proved in
eichhorn1991boundedness
.

It is clear that any compact manifold with any given metric will be of
k
-bounded geometry for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. A natural question would be whether this condition puts any constraint in the topology of the manifold. This is not the case.

Theorem 5.4.5
(
greene1978complete
).

Every differentiable manifold admits a complete metric of bounded geometry.

Given that we usually have access to upper and lower bounds on the sectional curvature, and given the form that takes the covariant derivatives in
Proposition

5.4.1
, we give the following definition, which is just a generalisation of that given in the introduction.

Definition 5.4.6
.

We say that a Riemannian manifold
(M,\textsl{g})
has
(\delta,\Delta,\Lambda)
-bounded geometry
if
\inj>0
and

\displaystyle\delta\leq\sec\leq\Delta

\displaystyle\lVert\lparen\nabla_{x}R\rparen(y,x)y+\lparen\nabla_{y}R\rparen\lparen y,x\rparen x\rVert\leq 2\Lambda\lVert x\rVert^{2}\lVert y\rVert^{2}\mathrlap{\qquad\forall p\in M\quad\forall x,y\in T_{p}M.}

Remark 5.4.7
.

If a manifold is of
(\delta,\Delta,\Lambda)
-bounded geometry according to
Definition

5.1.1
it is of
(\delta,\Delta,\Lambda)
-bounded geometry according to
Definition

5.4.6
, but not necessarily the other way around. In the proofs, we will just use
Definition

5.4.6
, as it is all we need. This definition also simplifies the computations when one wants to estimate the actual constant
\Lambda
for a concrete manifold.

Remark 5.4.8
(Injectivity radius).

We will not use the fact that these manifolds have uniformly bounded injectivity radius in this chapter, but this fact turns out to be crucial when proving other flavour of theorems in optimisation of manifolds that involve
\exp^{-1}_{p}
or parallel transport along geodesics or a retraction.

5.4.3

Curvature bounds

We start by recalling the following lemma that gives a closed formula for the curvature tensor of manifolds of constant sectional curvature. In its more general form, it says that the Riemannian curvature tensor is completely specified by the sectional curvature.

Lemma 5.4.9
(Riemann, 1854).

Let
(M,\textsl{g})
be a Riemannian manifold of constant sectional curvature
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The curvature tensor takes the form

R(x,y)z\coloneqq R_{\kappa}(x,y)z=\kappa\lparen\langle z,y\rangle x-\langle z,x\rangle y\rparen\mathrlap{\qquad\forall x,y,z\in T_{p}M.}

To be able to bound the terms in the differential equation, we will need some estimates on the norm of the curvature tensor in terms of the sectional curvature. We will use
4
inequalities that can be regarded as a generalisation of Berger’s lemma
berger1960sur
. The first three inequalities are announced—the last one with a worse constant—in
kaul1976schranken
citing for their proof a monograph by Karcher that was never published. We provide original proofs for these inequalities. The third inequality appears in
karcher1970short
, but with a different proof. The last inequality is entirely from
karcher1970short
.

Proposition 5.4.10
.

Let
(M,\textsl{g})
be a Riemannian manifold with bounded sectional curvature
\delta\leq\sec\leq\Delta
and define

\varepsilon=\frac{\Delta-\delta}{2}\qquad\mu=\frac{\Delta+\delta}{2}\qquad K=\max\{\lvert\Delta\rvert,\lvert\delta\rvert\}.

Given a point
p\in M
, we have the following inequalities

\displaystyle\lvert R(x,y,y,w)-R_{\mu}(x,y,y,w)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\lVert x\rVert\lVert y\rVert^{2}\lVert w\rVert\mathrlap{\qquad\forall x,y,w\in T_{p}M}

(5.10)

\displaystyle\lvert R(x,y,z,w)-R_{\mu}(x,y,z,w)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{3}\varepsilon\lVert x\rVert\lVert y\rVert\lVert z\rVert\lVert w\rVert\mathrlap{\quad\forall x,y,z,w\in T_{p}M}

(5.11)

The constants
1
and
\frac{4}{3}
are tight.

Furthermore, if
e\in T_{p}M
is a unitary vector, defining
R^{\perp}(x,y)z
as the component of the curvature tensor perpendicular to
e
, we have that for every
y,z
perpendicular to
e

\displaystyle\lVert R^{\perp}(e,y)z\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{3}\varepsilon\lVert y\rVert\lVert z\rVert

(5.12)

\displaystyle\lVert R(e,y)z\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2K.

(5.13)

Proof.

We may assume that the vectors
x,y,z,w
are of norm
1
by linearity. For (
5.10
), since the curvature tensor is skew-symmetric in the first two components and the last two components, we may assume that
x,w
are perpendicular to
y
. Fix a vector
y
and consider the bilinear form

T_{y}(x,w)=R(x,y,y,w)-R_{\mu}(x,y,y,w).

This application is symmetric, and as such, it attains its maximum at an eigenvector
x_{1}
of norm one orthogonal to
y
so that for every
x

\lvert T_{y}(x,w)\rvert\leq\lvert R(x_{1},y,y,x_{1})-R_{\mu}(x_{1},y,y,x_{1})\rvert=\lvert\sec(x_{1},y)-\mu\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

For the second inequality, by linearity we can assume that all the vectors have the same norm. By polarisation, we have that

\displaystyle 6R(x,y,z,w)

\displaystyle=R(x,y+z,y+z,w)-R(x,y-z,y-z,w)

\displaystyle-R(y,x+z,x+z,w)+R(y,x-z,x-z,w).

We then apply the triangle inequality to the expression for
(R-R_{\mu})(x,y,z,w)
together with (
5.10
) to get

6\lvert(R-R_{\mu})(x,y,z,w)\rvert\leq\varepsilon\Big\lparen\lVert x\rVert\lVert w\rVert\lparen\lVert y+z\rVert^{2}+\lVert y-z\rVert^{2}\rparen+\lVert y\rVert\lVert w\rVert\lparen\lVert x+z\rVert^{2}+\lVert x-z\rVert^{2}\rparen\Big\rparen

and using the parallelogram law together with the fact that all the vectors have the same norm, we get that

\lVert x+y\rVert^{2}+\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}=2(\lVert x\rVert^{2}+\lVert y\rVert^{2})=4\lVert x\rVert\lVert y\rVert

and the second inequality follows.

These two inequalities are tight on
\mathbb{C}P^{n}
seen as a real manifold
karcher1970short
.

The bound on the normal part of the curvature tensor follows directly from (
5.11
), since
R_{\mu}(e,y,z,w)=0
whenever
y,z,w
are perpendicular to
e
, so choosing
w
as the unitary vector in the direction of
R^{\perp}(e,y)z
we get that

\lVert R^{\perp}(e,y)z\rVert=\lvert R(e,y,z,w)\rvert\leq\frac{4}{3}\varepsilon\lVert y\rVert\lVert z\rVert.

The last inequality is proved in
karcher1970short
.
∎

5.4.4

A comparison lemma

We shall proceed in a very similar way to how we did in the case of the first order equation. We will simplify the differential equation to one in one dimension, and there, we will use a comparison theorem for functions of real variable. The only difference is that, in this case, we will not be able to simplify the computations to a Riccati equation. We start by proving the second order version of the Riccati comparison lemma but for the Jacobi equation. This lemma is stated in
kaul1976schranken
, but the reference given is in German and does not prove this inequality. We provide here a proof for completeness.

Lemma 5.4.11
(Jacobi comparison lemma).

Fix a real number
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
f,g\colon[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be two functions such that

\ddot{f}+\kappa f\leq\ddot{g}+\kappa g\mathrlap{\qquad f(0)=g(0),\dot{f}(0)\leq\dot{g}(0).}

Then
f\leq g
on
[0,\min\{r,\pi_{\kappa}\}]
.

Proof.

Let
h=g-f
and
\zeta=\ddot{h}+\kappa h\geq 0
. Let us show that the solution to the linear inhomogeneous initial value problem

\ddot{h}+\kappa h=\zeta\mathrlap{\qquad h(0)=0,\ \dot{h}(0)\geq 0}

is indeed positive on the given interval. Solving the equation, we find that the solution is given by

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle=\sn_{\kappa}(x)\int_{0}^{x}\sn^{\prime}_{\kappa}(t)\zeta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t-\sn_{\kappa}^{\prime}(x)\int_{0}^{x}\sn_{\kappa}(t)\zeta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\dot{h}(0)\sn_{\kappa}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x}\sn_{\kappa}(x-t)\zeta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\dot{h}(0)\sn_{\kappa}(x).

where we have used the trigonometric identity

\sn_{\kappa}(x-t)=\sn_{\kappa}(x)\sn^{\prime}_{\kappa}(t)-\sn^{\prime}_{\kappa}(x)\sn_{\kappa}(t).

From this we see that
h(x)\geq 0
on
[0,\min\{r,\pi_{\kappa}\}]
as
\sn_{\kappa}(x-t)
,
\zeta(t)
,
\dot{h}(0)
and
\sn_{\kappa}(x)
are positive in this interval.
∎

We can now show how to estimate the inhomogeneous differential equation for the Hessian of the exponential, taking advantage of the fact that the initial conditions are both zero.

Proposition 5.4.12
(Kaul, 1976).

Let
(M,\textsl{g})
be a Riemannian manifold with bounded sectional curvature
\delta\leq\sec\leq\Delta
.
Let
\gamma\colon[0,r]\to M
be a geodesic, and let
X,Y
be vector fields along
\gamma
with
X,Y\perp\dot{\gamma}
such that

\ddot{X}+R(X,\dot{\gamma})\dot{\gamma}=Y\mathrlap{\qquad X(0)=0,\dot{X}(0)=0.}

Assume that there exists a continuous
\eta
function such that
\lVert Y\rVert\leq\eta
on
[0,r]
. Then, we have that
\lVert X\rVert\leq\rho
on
[0,\min\{r,\pi_{\frac{\Delta+\delta}{2}}\}]
, where
\rho
is the solution of

\ddot{\rho}+\delta\rho=\eta\mathrlap{\qquad\rho(0)=0,\dot{\rho}(0)=0.}

Proof.

We define again the quantities

\varepsilon=\frac{\Delta-\delta}{2}\qquad\mu=\frac{\Delta+\delta}{2}.

Fix a
t_{0}\in[0,r]
and let
E
be the parallel vector field along
\gamma
such that
E(t_{0})=\frac{X(t_{0})}{\lVert X(t_{0})\rVert}
. The function
f=\langle X,E\rangle
satisfies the differential equation

wherewehaveused

Lemma

5.4.9
inthefirstequalityand

(
5.10
)
forthebound
.

Define
g
asthesolutiontothedifferentialequation$$\ddot{g}+\mu g=\lVert Y\rVert+\varepsilon\lVert X\rVert\mathrlap{\qquad g(0)=\dot{g}(0)=0.}$$Usingthat
f(t_0) = ∥X(t_0)∥
andthat
f(0) =
˙

f

(0) = 0
,by\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:comparison_edo},wegetthat
∥X(t_0)∥ ≤g(t_0)
,andsincethedefinitionof
g
doesnotdependonthechosen
t_0
,wemaydothisforany
t_0 ∈[0,r]
gettingthat
∥X∥ ≤g
on
[0, min{
π
_
μ
, r}]
.Now,Usingthat
∥X∥ ≤g
,wegetthat
g
satisfiestheinequality$$\ddot{g}+\delta g\leq\lVert Y\rVert\leq\eta.$$So,forthesolutionof$$\ddot{\rho}+\delta\rho=\eta\mathrlap{\qquad\rho(0)=0,\dot{\rho}(0)=0.}$$wehavethat
g ≤
η
on
[0, min{
π
_
δ
, r}] ⊇[0, min{
π
_
μ
, r}]
,gettingtheresult.\qed\end@proof\par\par

5.4.5
A second order version of Rauch’s theorem

We are now ready to give bounds on the Hessian of the exponential map. We first note that, since our goal is to bound the norm of the Hessian of
f\circ\exp_{p}
, as this Hessian is symmetric, it attains its maximum at an eigenvector. As such, we just need to bound the quantity

\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{v}(w,w)\mathrlap{\qquad v\in\segaux^{0}\lparen p\rparen,w\in T_{p}M.}

For this reason, we will start by giving bounds on the diagonal of the Hessian of the exponential map. We will then see that, since the Hessian is a symmetric bilinear map, we can leverage these bounds to give bounds on the full Hessian for any pair of vectors
w_{1},w_{2}\in T_{p}M
.

We give the second order bounds on the exponential map for a manifold of
(\delta,\Delta,\Lambda)
-bounded geometry (
cf
.,
Definition

5.4.6
).

Theorem 5.4.13
(Second order bounds for the exponential map).

Let
(M,\textsl{g})
be a Riemannian manifold with
(\delta,\Delta,\Lambda)
-bounded geometry. For a geodesic
\gamma\colon[0,r]\to M
with initial unit vector
v
, and a vector
w\in T_{p}M
, we have that

•

If
w
is radial to
\dot{\gamma}(0)
,

\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{rv}(w,w)=0.

•

If
w
is normal to
\dot{\gamma}(0)
, the radial part of the Hessian is bounded as

\Big\lparen\frac{1}{r}-\frac{\sn_{4\delta}(r)}{r^{2}}\Big\rparen\lVert w\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{rv}\lparen w,w\rparen,\dot{\gamma}(r)\rangle\leq\Big\lparen\frac{1}{r}-\frac{\sn_{4\Delta}(r)}{r^{2}}\Big\rparen\lVert w\rVert^{2}

for
r<\pi_{\Delta}
for the upper bound and
r<r_{\conjlocaux}\lparen v\rparen
for the lower bound.

The normal part of the Hessian is bounded for
r<\pi_{\frac{\Delta+\delta}{2}}
as

\lVert\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{rv}\lparen w,w\rparen^{\perp}\rVert\leq\rho(r)\lVert w\rVert^{2}

where

\rho(t)=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{8}{9r^{2}}$}\sn_{\delta}\big\lparen\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{t}{2}$}\big\rparen^{2}\big\lparen 3\Lambda\sn_{\delta}\big\lparen\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{t}{2}$}\big\rparen^{2}+2\lparen\Delta-\delta\rparen\sn_{\delta}\lparen t\rparen\big\rparen.

Both bounds and their radii are tight in spaces of constant curvature.

Furthermore, the radius of convergence for the normal part is tight for
\SOaux\lparen n\rparen
.

Proof.

As in
Proposition

5.4.1
, we write

\displaystyle\tilde{J}(t,s)

\displaystyle=\lparen\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{t(v+sw)}\Big\lparen\frac{t}{r}w\Big\rparen

\displaystyle J(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{J}(t,0)

\displaystyle K(t)

\displaystyle=\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{tv}\Big\lparen\frac{t}{r}w,\frac{t}{r}w\Big\rparen.

By linearity of the Hessian, it is enough to prove the result for
\lVert w\rVert=1
.

If
w
is radial, in
Proposition

5.4.1
we have that
Y=0
, so
K
is a solution of the equation

\ddot{K}+R(K,\dot{\gamma})\dot{\gamma}=0\mathrlap{\qquad K(0)=0,\dot{K}(0)=0}

and since it is a homogeneous second order linear equation with zero as the initial condition, its solution is
K(t)=0
for
t\in[0,r]
.

If
w
is normal, we need to bound the quantity
\nabla_{J}\tilde{J}
. Note that this is a vector field along
\gamma
, but to take the derivative in the direction of
J
we need to have
\tilde{J}
defined in that direction as well. We start by bounding its radial part

\langle\nabla_{J}\tilde{J},\nabla r\rangle=D_{J}\langle\tilde{J},\nabla r\rangle-\langle\tilde{J},\nabla_{J}\nabla r\rangle=D_{J}\langle\tilde{J},\nabla r\rangle-\Hess r(J,J).

(5.14)

We can compute the first term directly. By Gauss’s lemma we can simplify this derivative to one on
T_{p}M

D_{J}\langle\tilde{J},\nabla r\rangle(\gamma(t))=\frac{1}{r}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right_{s=0}\Big\langle\frac{t}{r}w,\frac{t\lparen v+sw\rparen}{\lVert t\lparen v+sw\rparen\rVert_{T_{p}M}}\Big\rangle_{T_{p}M}=\frac{t}{r^{2}}.

Using the bounds on the Hessian of the distance function given in
we can bound the second term in (
5.14
). Evaluating these two quantities at
r
and using the bounds on the size of the Jacobi fields together with the trigonometric identity

\sn_{\kappa}(t)\sn^{\prime}_{\kappa}(t)=\frac{\sn_{\kappa}(2t)}{2}=\sn_{4\kappa}(t)

(5.15)

we get the bounds on the tangential part of the Hessian of the exponential.

Finally, for its normal part, consider the differential equation given by
Proposition

5.4.1
. Since
\langle K,\dot{\gamma}\rangle^{\prime}=\langle\dot{K},\dot{\gamma}\rangle
, the radial (resp. normal) part of the derivative is the derivative of the radial (resp. normal) part. For this reason, we have that

\lparen K^{\perp}\rparen^{\prime\prime}+R(K^{\perp},\dot{\gamma})\dot{\gamma}=-Y^{\perp}.

Therefore, we just have to bound
\lVert Y^{\perp}\rVert
to be able to use
Proposition

5.4.12
and finish.

We start by giving a bound on the norm of
\dot{J}
. Using that
\dot{J}=\nabla_{J}\nabla r=\Hess r(J)
,

\lVert\dot{J}\rVert\leq\lVert\Hess r\rVert\lVert J\rVert.

(5.16)

We can then bound the norm of
Y^{\perp}
as

\displaystyle\lVert Y^{\perp}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\lparen\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}R\rparen\lparen J,\dot{\gamma}\rparen J+\lparen\nabla_{J}R\rparen\lparen J,\dot{\gamma}\rparen\dot{\gamma}\rVert+4\lVert R^{\perp}\lparen J,\dot{\gamma}\rparen\dot{J}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2\Lambda\lVert J\rVert^{2}+\frac{8(\Delta-\delta)}{3}\lVert J\rVert\lVert\dot{J}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\Big\lparen 2\Lambda+\frac{8(\Delta-\delta)}{3}\ct_{\delta}(t)\Big\rparen\lVert J\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\lparen 2\Lambda+\frac{8(\Delta-\delta)}{3}\ct_{\delta}(t)\Big\rparen\frac{\sn_{\delta}(t)^{2}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r^{2}}\Big\lparen\Lambda\sn_{\delta}(t)^{2}+\frac{2(\Delta-\delta)}{3}\sn_{\delta}(2t)\Big\rparen.

where we have used (
5.12
)—given that since
J
is perpendicular to
\dot{\gamma}
, so is
\dot{J}
—to bound the normal part of the curvature tensor. We have also used the first order bound on the differential of the exponential for perpendicular initial conditions to bound the norm of the Jacobi fields and (
5.16
) to bound their derivative. In the last equality we have used (
5.15
) again.

The result follows by noting that
\rho
is the solution to the differential equation

\ddot{\rho}+\delta\rho=\eta\mathrlap{\qquad\rho(0)=\dot{\rho}(0)=0}

where
\eta
is the bound on
\lVert Y^{\perp}\rVert
and applying
Proposition

5.4.12
.

We will see that the radius for the bound of the normal part is tight in the case of
\SOaux\lparen n\rparen
in
Example

5.5.4
.
∎

The first thing to note is that these bounds go to zero as
r
tends to zero. This is exactly what we expect, as the Christoffel symbols in normal coordinates vanish at the origin.

The bounds in this theorem provide a notable improvement compared to the best bounds previously known (
cf
.,
kaul1976schranken
). For one, these bounds are tighter. We also have that these bounds are explicit, compared to the previous bounds, which were given in terms of a solution of a differential equation that did not have an explicit integral. We also simplified the technical tools necessary to get to these bounds.

We finish this section by giving a bound on the full Hessian of the exponential.

Theorem 5.4.14
(Bounds on the Full Hessian).

Let
(M,\textsl{g})
be a Riemannian manifold with
(\delta,\Delta,\Lambda)
-bounded geometry. For a geodesic
\gamma\colon[0,r]\to M
with initial unit vector
v
,
r<\pi_{\frac{\Delta+\delta}{2}}
, and any two vectors
w_{1},w_{2}\in T_{p}M
, we have that

\lVert\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{rv}\lparen w_{1},w_{2}\rparen\rVert\leq\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{8}{3r^{2}}$}\sn_{\delta}\big\lparen\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{r}{2}$}\big\rparen^{2}\lparen\Lambda\sn_{\delta}\big\lparen\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{r}{2}$}\big\rparen^{2}+2\max\{\lvert\Delta\rvert,\lvert\delta\rvert\}\sn_{\delta}\lparen r\rparen\rparen\lVert w_{1}\rVert\lVert w_{2}\rVert.

Furthermore, the radius
\pi_{\frac{\Delta+\delta}{2}}
is tight for
\SOaux\lparen n\rparen
.

Proof.

To bound the full Hessian we first see that it is just enough to bound its diagonal part. For any symmetric bilinear form and any two vectors we have the polarisation formula

4\Phi(u,v)=\Phi(u+v,u+v)-\Phi(u-v,u-v).

By linearity, we may assume that
\lVert u\rVert=\lVert v\rVert=1
. Taking absolute values and applying the triangle inequality and Cauchy–Schwarz, we get that

4\lVert\Phi(u,v)\rVert\leq\lVert\Phi_{\operatorname{diag}}\rVert\lparen\lVert u+v\rVert^{2}+\lVert u-v\rVert^{2}\rparen=4\lVert\Phi_{\operatorname{diag}}\rVert

where
\lVert\Phi_{\operatorname{diag}}\rVert
is the operator norm of the application
u\mapsto\Phi(u,u)
. For this reason, it is enough to bound the map
u\mapsto\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{tv}\lparen u,u\rparen
.

Let
w
be a vector normal to
v
. As we did in
Theorem

5.4.13
, we consider the differential equation for

K(t)=\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{tv}\Big\lparen\frac{t}{r}w,\frac{t}{r}w\Big\rparen.

This is exactly the same differential equation that we had for
K^{\perp}
, only that rather than having to bound the normal part of the curvature tensor, we bound the full curvature tensor. For that we use (
5.13
), and following with the bounds as we did in
Theorem

5.4.13
and solving the resulting equation we get that for
w
perpendicular to
v
, the norm of
K
is bounded by the solution of the differential equation

\ddot{\rho}+\delta\rho=\frac{2}{r^{2}}\lparen\Lambda\sn_{\delta}(t)^{2}+2\max\{\lvert\Delta\rvert,\lvert\delta\rvert\}\sn_{\delta}(2r)\rparen\mathrlap{\qquad\rho(0)=\dot{\rho}(0)=0}

which is solved by

\rho(t)=\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{8}{3r^{2}}$}\sn_{\delta}\big\lparen\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{t}{2}$}\big\rparen^{2}\lparen\Lambda\sn_{\delta}\big\lparen\mbox{\small$\displaystyle\frac{t}{2}$}\big\rparen^{2}+2\max\{\lvert\Delta\rvert,\lvert\delta\rvert\}\sn_{\delta}\lparen t\rparen\rparen.\qed

Remark 5.4.15
(Tighter bounds).

Another way to obtain bounds on the full Hessian of the exponential would be to take the bounds from
Theorem

5.4.13
for the normal and parallel part of the Hessian and using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to get bounds of the form

\lVert\lparen\nabla\mathrm{d}\exp_{p}\rparen_{rv}\lparen w_{1},w_{2}\rparen\rVert\leq\sqrt{\sigma^{2}+\rho^{2}}\lVert w_{1}\rVert\lVert w_{2}\rVert.

Where
\rho
is the bound of the normal part defined in
Theorem

5.4.13
and

\sigma=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\max\{\lvert r-\sn_{4\delta}(r)\rvert,\lvert r-\sn_{4\Delta}(r)\rvert\}

that is,
\sigma
is the bound on the norm of the parallel part of the Hessian.

This bound, although tighter in most specific examples, might be more difficult to manipulate and lacks the simplicity of that presented in the theorem above.

5.5
Concrete Second Order Bounds

5.5.1
Constant curvature

The simplest family to evaluate these bounds in is that of the spaces of constant curvature such as the sphere, the hyperbolic plane, the Euclidean space or the flat torus.

For a manifold of constant curvature
\kappa
, since the curvature is constant, the derivative of the curvature tensor is zero. If we further assume that their injectivity radius is positive

(\kappa,\kappa,0)

5.5.2
Locally symmetric spaces

Locally symmetric spaces define a large family of particularly well-behaved Riemannian manifolds. Examples of these spaces are the flat torus, the orthogonal group (or any compact Lie group with a bi-invariant metric), the space of symmetric positive definite matrices, the Grassmannian, the oriented Grassmannian and the hyperbolic Grassmannian.
3
3

3

All these manifolds are to be regarded as Riemannian manifolds with the metric inherited from their quotient structure

We recall the algebraic definition of a locally symmetric space.

Definition 5.5.1
.

A Riemannian manifold
(M,\textsl{g})
is
locally symmetric
if the curvature tensor is covariantly constant, that is,
\nabla R=0
.

Locally symmetric spaces were introduced and studied by Cartan in 1926, who also gave a complete classification of these in 1932.

The most notable examples of locally symmetric spaces are symmetric spaces which are one of the most important families of real manifolds in Riemannian geometry. These were intensively studied by Sigurður Helgason
helgason1978differential
.

Definition 5.5.2
.

A Riemannian manifold
(M,\textsl{g})
is a
symmetric space
if, for every point
p\in M
there exists an involutive isometry
\sigma_{p}
that fixes
p
, that is

\sigma_{p}(p)=p\qquad\lparen\mathrm{d}\sigma_{p}\rparen_{0}=-\mathrm{Id}.

As the name implies, one may prove that symmetric spaces are indeed locally symmetric spaces.

For these manifolds we have the following result.

Proposition 5.5.3
.

A symmetric space with bounded sectional curvature
\delta\leq\sec\leq\Delta
is of
(\delta,\Delta,0)
-bounded geometry.

Proof.

Since it is a locally symmetric space, we have that
\nabla R=0
, so the first order bound is clear. Since symmetric spaces are Riemannian homogeneous spaces, for every two points there exists an isometry taking one to the other. As such, the injectivity radius is constant throughout the manifold and thus, positive.
∎

From this, we get that we just need to compute bounds on the sectional curvature for these manifolds in order to give second order bounds for the exponential map. Now, bounds on the sectional curvature of these manifolds are well-known. We give here some examples that are particularly useful in the context of optimisation.

Example 5.5.4
(The special orthogonal group).

The sectional curvature for a Lie group with a bi-invariant metric, after identifying any pair of tangent vector with vectors in the Lie algebra, is given for a pair of orthonormal vectors
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}

\sec(X,Y)=\frac{1}{4}\lVert[X,Y]\rVert^{2}.

In the special case of
\SOaux\lparen n\rparen
for
n>2
, we have that
\mathfrak{g}\cong\Skewaux\lparen n\rparen
.
It is clear that the sectional curvature is non-negative for any bi-invariant metric. In the case of
\SOaux\lparen n\rparen
this bound is tight.

Consider the bi-invariant metric given by the Frobenius norm—the metric inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For the upper bounds, if we work with a matrix Lie group, it is enough to bound the norm of
XY-YX
for matrices
X,Y
of Frobenius norm
1
. In the general case, this inequality is called the Böttcher–Wenzel inequality and it reads

\lVert[X,Y]\rVert\leq 2\lVert X\rVert\lVert Y\rVert\mathrlap{\qquad\forall X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}.}

For a review of this inequality and a particularly clean proof see
lu2012remarks
.

For the case of
\SOaux\lparen n\rparen
, that is, when
X,Y
are skew-symmetric, this inequality can be improved
bloch2005commutators

\lVert[X,Y]\rVert\leq\lVert X\rVert\lVert Y\rVert\mathrlap{\qquad\forall X,Y\in\Skewaux\lparen n\rparen.}

For
n=3
the constant can be further improved to
\frac{1}{2}
. These constants are tight.

Wrapping all this together, we get that the bounds for
\SOaux\lparen n\rparen
with
n>2
are given by

.\end{example}\par Thesesameideascanbegeneralisedtosymmetricspaces.WewillusethistocomputeboundsfortheHessianoftheexponentialmapintheGrassmannian.\par\begin{example}Let $G/H$ be a symmetric space. Since a symmetric space is a Riemannian homogeneous space, we just need to bound the sectional point at one point, as the space has the same sectional curvature at every point. Denote by $\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}$ the orthogonal complement of the Lie algebra of $H$ with respect to the metric at the identity in $G$. This complement to $\mathfrak{h}$ is not a Lie algebra itself and in fact $[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}]\subseteq\mathfrak{h}$, as $G/H$ is a symmetric space. This set $\mathfrak{m}$ may be identified isometrically with the tangent space at $\pi(e)$, the projection of the identity element $e\in G$, in other words, the map
$$\lparen\mathrm{d}\pi\rparen_{e}{}_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon\mathfrak{m}\to T_{\pi(e)}G/H$$is a linear isometry. Via this identification we may treat vectors $X,Y\in T_{\pi(e)}G/H$ as vectors $\overline{X},\overline{Y}\in\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}$. Now, by O'Neill's formula\penalty\ \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{oneill1966fundamental}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Ch.\penalty\ $7$, Thm.\penalty\ $47$}, the sectional curvature for these manifolds has a particularly simple formula for orthonormal vectors $X,Y\in T_{\pi(e)}G/H$
$$\sec_{G/H}(X,Y)=\sec_{G}(\overline{X},\overline{Y})+\frac{3}{4}\lVert[\overline{X},\overline{Y}]\rVert^{2}$$where we have used that $[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}]\subseteq\mathfrak{h}$.
\par If $G$ is a Lie group with a bi-invariant metric we say that $G/H$ is a {normal symmetric space}. For a normal metric, the sectional curvature simplifies to
$$\sec_{G/H}(X,Y)=\lVert[\overline{X},\overline{Y}]\rVert^{2}.$$Trough the study of symmetric spaces of non-compact type and their duality, it is not difficult to prove that the sectional curvature on any symmetric space is either the norm or minus the norm of the Lie bracket of a pair of vectors, although we will not prove that as we will not need it.
\end{example}\par\begin{example}The real Grassmannian as a symmetric space is given by the quotient $\Graux\lparen n,k\rparen=\SOaux\lparen n\rparen/\lparen\SOaux\lparen k\rparen\times\SOaux\lparen n-k\rparen\rparen$, where the metric on $\SOaux\lparen n\rparen$ is the bi-invariant metric generated by the scalar product $\langle X,Y\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\tr\lparen X^{\intercal}Y\rparen$ on the Lie algebra. For this symmetric space we have that
\@@amsgather
